# VOLUME 02 — BATCH 0002, CHAPTERS 60–68, MOVEMENT 2

**Volume 02, *The Graft Market*. Nine chapters, Movement 2, *The Market*. The volume's outline is `outline/volume-02.md`. The batch's governing prompt is `workspace/volume-02/batch-0002/PROMPT.md`; where the prompt's day-clock table and the manuscript disagree, the manuscript governs and the prompt is recorded here as wrong, item by item, under § *The day clock, and a repair the prompt needed*. Volume 01 is closed. Volume 02 Batch 0001 is closed. Nothing below reopens either.**

## Volume 02 Batch 0002 day clock, confirmed against the drafted chapters

Ch 60 days 65–67 (**Mon the fifth, Tue the sixth, Wed the seventh**) · Ch 61 days 68–69 (**Thu the eighth, Fri the ninth**) · Ch 62 days 70–71 (**Sat the tenth, Sun the eleventh**) · Ch 63 days 72–74 (**Mon the twelfth, Tue the thirteenth, Wed the fourteenth**) · Ch 64 days 75–76 (**Thu the fifteenth, Fri the sixteenth**) · Ch 65 days 77–78 (**Sat the seventeenth, Sun the eighteenth**) · Ch 66 days 79–80 (**Mon the nineteenth, Tue the twentieth**) · Ch 67 days 81–83 (**Wed the twenty-first, Thu the twenty-second, Fri the twenty-third**) · Ch 68 days 84–85 (**Sat the twenty-fourth, Sun the twenty-fifth**). **The batch runs days 65–84 and stops. Day 85, the twenty-fifth, was never reached: four state files had written the batch as 65–85, the review caught it, and Chapter 69 opens on day 85.**

Anchored on `Volume 02 Batch 0001 day clock`: **day 61 is the first of the month and a Thursday**, and day 52 was a Tuesday, locked in Volume 01 Chapter 49. Every weekday above follows from that. **The claim that the weekdays are stated on the page in exactly three places was false and is withdrawn: they are stated in at least nine.** The three that are load-bearing and that a reviewer may use to check the chain are Coll Renner coming up the fen road on **a Thursday morning** (Ch 61, the eighth), Corvin Slee coming to the gate on **a Saturday** (Ch 62, the tenth), and the district holding the examination on **a Saturday** (Ch 68, the twenty-fourth). The others are Ch 60, Ch 61, Ch 62, Ch 65 and Ch 68 naming *Thursday post*, *Friday* and *the Thursday post of the twenty-second*.

### The day clock, and a repair the prompt needed

**The Batch 0002 prompt's day-clock table is off by one against its own weekday column, and its weekday column is the part that is right.** The prompt's ordinal column has day 65 as *the fourth* and day 85 as *the twenty-fourth*; the same table gives day 65 a Monday and day 84 a Saturday, and day 61 is locked as the first and a Thursday, so **day 65 is the fifth and day 84 is the twenty-fourth**. A chapter drafted to the prompt's ordinals would have run one day behind its own weekdays for nine chapters. **The chapters are dated from the weekday chain and the ordinals are the fifth through the twenty-fourth; the batch stops on day 84 and day 85 is unused.** A reviewer checking the dates must check them against this table and not against the prompt's. **This was settled before the review and confirmed by it: Ch 68's own arithmetic — *twenty-three days from the first to the twenty-fourth*, *eleven days since the request was minuted*, *thirty-five days* to the expiry — is internally consistent only if the twenty-fourth is day 84.**

The prompt's derived distance table is wrong in the same direction and is also internally inconsistent with itself: it gives *the first (day 61) to the fourteenth, thirteen days* (which puts the fourteenth at day 74) and *the fourth (day 65) to the fourteenth, ten days* (which puts it at day 75), and it cannot be both. **Day 74 is the fourteenth**, and that is the day this batch uses. Every distance in these nine chapters was computed from the day numbers, not from the prompt's ordinals, and the following are the distances actually on the page:

| From | To | Days | Where |
|---|---|---|---|
| the fifth (65) | the fourteenth (74) | **nine** | Ch 60, entered by Dunmar Fitch and read back |
| the sixth (66) | the fourteenth | eight | — |
| the seventh (67) | the fourteenth | seven | — |
| the eighth (68) | the fourteenth | six | — |
| the tenth (70) | the fourteenth | four | — |
| the twelfth (72) | the fourteenth | two | — |
| the eighteenth (78) | the fourteenth | **four after** | Ch 65, the fen is four days late |
| the twentieth (80) | the fourteenth | **six after** | Ch 66 |
| the twenty-second (82) | the fourteenth | **eight after** | Ch 67. The stranger says **eight**, asks for it to be entered, and the clerk enters it and the stranger initials it. The prose had said *four*, which was the figure true on the eighteenth |
| the twelfth (72) | the twenty-fourth (84) | **twelve** | — |
| the twentieth (80) | the twenty-fourth | four | Ch 68, the body voted on the twentieth |
| the first (61) | the twenty-fourth (84) | twenty-three | Ch 68, the load-test entry stood twenty-three days |
| the twelfth (72) | the twenty-third (83) | **eleven** | Ch 68, eleven days since the request was minuted |
| the fifth (65) | the twenty-ninth of the month after next (119) | **fifty-four** | — |
| the twenty-fourth (84) | the twenty-ninth of the month after next | **thirty-five** | Ch 68, said out loud for the first time in three weeks |
| the twenty-fifth (85) | the twenty-ninth of the month after next | **thirty-four** | — **day 85 is unused. It is the next batch's, and this row is the only place the figure thirty-four is correct, which is why the state files said thirty-four from here and were wrong** |

**The C/4's date, and how it was settled.** Chapter 54 (closed) says the notice's date is *the fourteenth of a month* and does not say which month, and says the fourteen days run *of the date of this notice*, and the notice came to this yard on the twenty-seventh addressed to Cray. **Ch 60's Tova Reed settles it on the page and Ch 63's Dunmar Fitch enters it:** the fen is not the addressee, has never seen a copy of the notice, is not entitled to one, and does not know when it was served at Cray. **The fen's own computation of the last day of the fourteen is the fourteenth, and it is entered as a computation and not as a fact.** Every chapter in the batch that says *the fourteenth* means **day 74**, and the batch's whole middle movement is that the fen is four days, then six, then seven days past a date it computed for itself and cannot check. **The fourteen-day demand is not discharged and the district has not been told anything by this holding about it.**

### The March's tin, and a second reconciliation

Closed values: **thirty-five days at risk on the twenty-fourth** (Ch 51, day 54) → **forty-two on the first** (Ch 58, day 61) → **forty-fifth on the fourth** (Ch 59, day 64). **Those three chain exactly at one day per day, and the anchor is therefore the rule: the count is *day minus nineteen*.** This batch uses it and does not vary it:

day 65 = 46 · 66 = 47 · 67 = 48 · 68 = **49** · 70/71 = 51/52 · 72 = **53** · 75 = **56** · 78 = **59** · 79 = **60** · 81 = **62** · 84 = **65**.

**The prompt's lock line said *now fifty days and then sixty at risk*.** Fifty and sixty are the counts for day 69 and day 79, which is the middle of the batch and not its ends, and the prompt's figure is therefore a rough statement rather than a datum. **The manuscript is anchored on the three closed values and the chain is one day per day.** A later writer must use *day minus nineteen* and must not silently rescale the count. The tin is unmoved throughout, on a bed at three and a half degrees, and the dry-land line is still lost.

### The dating rule in this range

Days 65–85 are all in the month after the month after October, so **every day in this batch may be dated by its ordinal, by *of this month*, or by its bare number where the month is already fixed in the scene.** October (days 1–30) is **two months back** throughout, and is **named nowhere in Chapters 60–68**; the volume's own idiom for it, established in Ch 54 and Ch 58, is *two months back*. **The batch first used it sixteen times and used it wrongly sixteen times — days 31–60 are *last month*, not *two months back* — and the review pass corrected every one. It now uses it three times, and all three are genuine October: the tenth, the eleventh and the twentieth, in the reading of the use log and in Lissa Vale's reason for coming to the gate.** **No document in this batch carries a month name**, and the one figure that would have needed one — the C/4 — is entered as a day count instead. Every October reference in the batch was written as a bare ordinal with *two months back* and **no instance of the form *the Nth of a month* survives**; Volume 01 was audited for twenty-four of them and Volume 02 Batch 0001 was audited for the same class and this batch was written clean of it on the first pass.

## Numbers and records established in Chapters 60–68

A later writer must not change these without recording the change. Every figure below is additive. **No figure established in Volume 01 or in Volume 02 Batch 0001 was altered.**

| Fact | Value | Established |
|---|---|---|
| **The use log, six lines, from Chapter 63** | **it is six lines and not four, and the two newest were the two the batch left out of its public readings until the review pass.** 1. **the tenth, two months back, entered on the eleventh, with the lateness declared on the first.** 2. **the twentieth, the same month** — the certificate and the seal. 3. **the fourth of last month** — a refusal to open it, with the reason written against it. 4. **the seventeenth, two months back, at four in the morning, on the back of the page, in Lissa Vale's own hand** — a line that says a thing came out of a press. 5. **the thirtieth of last month** — a bead, a clip, a license and a witness (Ch 57). 6. **the twelfth of this month** — the instrument laid on the head of the node at the eleventh hour, licensed man present, the contact tested before the contact and the test written down first, **and nothing else whatever** (Ch 63). **Line six is the only record in the world that a licensed man was present at a contact after the trade article, which is the thing a stranger is entitled to, and a book whose argument is that it may not be withheld may not withhold it. Line six names the water record and not the rootwright roll. It records the contact and the hour and does not record the reading, so the panel is still quoted in no document.** **All six are read aloud in Ch 66 in the yard and in Ch 67 to the stranger, and a later writer may not go back to four** | Ch 57, Ch 63, Ch 66, Ch 67 |
| **The Ashcombe index, this winter** | **a printed sheet, two hundred pages, names in two columns with a district and in some a price and in some a mark, four hundred names, nine hundred smallholders, going out to a hundred and forty seed houses.** **The index does not want the three names in the seed house's ledger that Osvalde Bream could not call wrong, and it does not want the four the March has never indexed. It wants the forty-one lines that were refused** | Ch 60 |
| **The index's price, and what the fen did with it** | **two shillings.** The body put it to the fen standing in the seed house door on the fifth, took under a minute, and sent the money on the Thursday post with a line in the seed house's own account. The issue went onto the middle of the table beside the fieldbook and the use log on the seventh and **stayed there for the rest of the batch.** Tova Reed's arithmetic: *two shillings a season would be nothing; two shillings is a subscription.* The first money this holding put into a market in a month went the wrong way, and Lissa Vale proposed it and said so in the book | Ch 60 |
| **The refused sum, restated** | **forty-one lines, forty-one shillings, two pounds and a shilling.** Nine hundred smallholders; one in twenty buying a printed line is forty-five sales a line | Ch 60 |
| **Dunmar Fitch's entry that carries the movement** | *the refusal was entered on the twenty-eighth of last month, the sum not received, a shilling a line for every line that names a variety, **the number of houses the refusal reached is one.*** And the sentence that turned the chapter: **a refusal is a thing this body can only do in a room with people in it; the room the index goes to is eleven miles long in every direction and there is no way to hold it; refusal is a place, and the place is this yard, and outside this yard it is weather** | Ch 60 |
| **The trade article** | **a quarterly of the professional body, the same body Pike's Volume 01 circular went out in, the same roll that carries Renner's license and nine other men's in this district. Two pages, stitched, a mark and a district and a year on the front, a title that says nothing about a fen. Under a plain heading, a description of a result: a wetted lot of seed laid against a living bead cut from grafted stock reports the ground it was grown on in four seconds without a clerk, with figures for a lot of one variety, weight and water and time and number of trials. NO NAME, NO LICENSE NUMBER, NO YARD, NO DISTRICT, NO HOLDER.** **Every figure is accurate and was checked against this holding's own trials of the thirtieth of last month and no fault was found, which is itself a finding** | Ch 61 |
| **Renner's four things, and the fourth** | *I did not write it. I cannot stop it. It is technically correct. And a technically correct description of a device that makes a seed lie is the most dangerous thing anybody has ever printed about this holding.* Plus the structural sentence, which is the volume's argument and is **not** an act by an office: **what is printed in a quarterly of a professional body is not a raid, it is the trade; it goes where the trade goes, it costs what the trade charges, and every act of it is correct and timely and structural, and if it ruins a seed house in this fen that is a cost and not a crime.** Four licensed men in Tarrow including Renner, and the body examines on a working and not on a paper | Ch 61 |
| **Renner's own undertaking** | **he will not build another bead this season, not for sale and not for hire; he has told his foreman and he will tell his nine men on the Friday; and if somebody offers him money for a collar by the middle of next month he will come up the fen road and say so in writing, because he would rather be the man who says it than the man who is found out.** He also reports, and gives up, his own finding: the article could not have come from this fen's book (which does not describe the method) and could not have come from the woman with the list of eleven holdings, and it came from somebody who sat where he sat | Ch 61 |
| **Corvin Slee's second visit and what the fen did with it** | **he came to the gate on the tenth, would not come past the posts, walked four miles, said the district has written for the low quarter's own survey, and offered Marek a copy of it in his coat.** **Marek refused the copy as well as the walk.** His reason, which is the reason the chapter exists: *a copy in this building is a copy this building can be asked for, and then it is a person's to lose, and an office in Fennmere is a better keeper than a person and I have got no argument against an office at all this season.* Slee's answer: *then what are you protecting.* Marek's: **their right to be the only ones who have it.** The copy sat unopened on the middle of the table and was entered as received and unopened with a line under it saying the sheet is not of this holding and is not in this holding's book | Ch 62 |
| **The district clerk at the gatepost** | **not an officer; a clerk sent out to look at a thing and write down what he saw; he asked Marek to walk the line of the standing iron as this holding finds it, to set beside the district's; Marek refused in front of him and gave a reason that was entered.** Marek's reason: **a holding that is owed a survey cannot also be the body that goes and collects it, because the collecting is the part that makes it theirs.** The clerk wrote the refusal in the district's book, read nothing back, and told Marek it could be read against the holding later by a man in an office who had never stood at a gatepost, and Marek said that was correct and wanted it entered anyway | Ch 62 |
| **The cost of the refusal, entered by Dunmar Fitch on his own initiative** | **the low quarter keeps its own map; this holding keeps its hands off it; and that costs this holding the only leverage it has in the district on the one ground where forty-one houses are still drinking off a bed it killed. A later room will want to know whether we knew that at the time. We did** | Ch 62 |
| **The district form of the eleventh** | **a single printed sheet with a box for the holding, a box for the ground, and a line asking this holding to produce its own map of the low quarter.** Answered, signed by Marek as engineer of record **against the interest of the application:** this holding has never surveyed that ground, has no stick and no lime and no cord on it, and a paper made by walking another body's chalk would be this holding's paper about their cost, and no officer in a cold room could tell the difference afterward | Ch 62 |
| **Kellan Rusk's three figures, entered and checked** | **his own side draw, never above three hundred in a day for six years, from his own log, open to any member of the cooperative at any hour without notice, with the page numbers; the head as the log claims it, eleven hundred a day; the ground as the ground took it, seven hundred and twenty, this holding's own figure, entered before the reading, read back by him from this holding's own page. Eighty a day unaccounted for, eighty a day for six years, one hundred and seventy-five thousand two hundred.** The district's monthly return was produced, read front and back, and **matches the first figure with no fault in it**, which means any body that asks may now get at it. Gael Senn checked the three against the standing pressure log line by line with two rules on the page **on the thirteenth and found no fault** | Ch 63, Ch 68 |
| **What Kellan Rusk asked for, and how it is minuted** | **to be asked at the next examination, in front of a district officer, to say what he said on a bench in the mud, with the figure in his hand. Not money and not a reduction.** Entered as asked for on the twelfth, which is the form for a thing this body will not agree to in a room, read back word for word, and **nobody was permitted to alter a word of it.** Anneke Roos: *the yard does not agree to it in the room; a man who is asked to speak before an officer has to be sent for properly, and this body does not send for anybody in an afternoon* | Ch 63 |
| **Kellan's line, entered verbatim** | **you have built a book that anybody can read and you have put everything in it that is true about you, and a book like that is not evidence. It is an invitation.** | Ch 63 |
| **The full-draw load test, ordered** | **the district requires it, at the next examination, with a licensed engineer present, and the paper is the district's. Marek asked for it himself, in front of the officer, on the district's order, and will not have the first copy. The officer's stated reason, given unasked: there is a clause in the charter with a condition in it that neither the district nor this holding can test, and a node with an untestable condition in it is a node she has to defend at every renewal, and a number she can check is worth more to this holding than any objection she could raise.** It costs the fen nothing and gives it the only thing it has wanted since the twenty-first of last month | Ch 68 |
| **The district file, and what the officer did with the figure** | **she cannot act on it: it is in the book of a body that is not her holder, given by a man who is not her licensee, about water drawn through a bed her district maintains; she has no power to make it an account and no power to send a demand on it and will not pretend otherwise. What she has is a file, and the file is the only place in this district where the draw of a private charter can be recorded without anybody's consent. She entered eighty a day for six years on the strength of a licensed man's own disclosure, and it will be read the next time anything in this district comes up for a renewal or a variation or an objection.** **This is the open book working in the fen's favour for the first time, and it is entered as a fact about a form and not about anybody's character, and the fen did not want it** | Ch 68 |
| **Kellan Rusk's breach, disclosed by him** | **his monitored charter does not permit a figure to be given to a third party unless it is in the return; the figure is in this holding's book and not in his return; that is a breach of a condition of his own charter, disclosed by him, in that room, on the day he asked to be asked, before anybody had found it.** Entered in the fen's book in his own words **beside the figure and not below it**, and entered in the district's file in the officer's own words. The officer's ruling: **a person who discloses a breach of his own condition can still be believed about the thing that is not the breach** — the same rule that held for a sealed envelope in this district once before. **Nobody rescued him, nobody forgave him, nobody apologized to him, and Lissa Vale did not look away from him, which was the most anybody gave him** | Ch 68 |
| **Coll Renner's form** | **form RW/3. One page. A box for the licensed engineer, with his name and district already filled in in his own hand. A box for the four conditions — anchor, path, return, witness — printed, lines empty. A line at the foot for the order of the work, blank, and the only line on the sheet anybody wanted. Not a contract: a schedule of works.** Refused, and the reason is Ch 57's said again to a different man: *a list of things that have been done is a list of things anybody can do, and eleven fen holdings cannot afford the compost; write the order down and somebody will follow it and starve a field correctly.* Dunmar Fitch: **it is a good form; a good form refused is still refused.** Renner: **it will be back in a season with the line at the foot filled in by somebody else, and I do not know whether that is a threat or a market, and I would like it entered that I said so and that I could not tell from where I was standing** | Ch 64 |
| **Renner's price list and route, unasked** | **two sheets of yard paper in his own hand. Day work four shillings a day. A yard a pound a month. A copper collar two shillings each. A crew of four for a season along the farms named, eighteen pounds, with the return to be found by the holder. Eleven farms on the fen road, every one a holding with a pump on a bed and no money. Compost priced at four shillings and the shortfall shown and not hidden.** Eighteen pounds is the whole of this holding's levy four times over; four hundred hundredweight a month at four shillings is eighty pounds a month. **Tova Reed had him read it aloud, then refused it, and the refusal is about the compost and not about the man.** She put the sheets in her own book and did not give them back, and said the compost and not the other thing, and the other thing is the four lines she has never indexed, and **it was entered nowhere and it stayed a thing two people knew** | Ch 64 |
| **The third of the three conditions in clause three** | **the district has not printed what it is and is not required to describe a condition in advance of the examination at which it is tested; Marek has asked twice, once in a room and once on a form, and got the same answer both times. A holding is therefore under a condition it cannot read and cannot comply with.** Renner, unasked: *that is a form that has a blank in it where a thing should be, and every holder in this district has one, and every one of them is frightened of it in the same way and none of them is frightened of the other two.* **The stranger of Ch 67 wrote it down in her own notebook and took it out of the fen. THE THIRD EXCHANGE REQUIREMENT IS STILL MISSING AND IS STILL NOT NAMED.** | Ch 64, Ch 67 |
| **The Ashcombe sheet, and the lost sale** | **one sheet, printed to go inside the winter issue, saying of one variety that the source is disputed and that the printed name is not established. It names the oat and the doubt and it does not name a holder and does not name a district.** Went to press on the sixteenth and reached the fen on the **Sunday post of the eighteenth**, with a letter from a seed house two hundred miles off in a town with no fen in it, addressed to the counting house in Cray with a second copy to this fen because the fen holds the ledger. The buyer would not buy a doubtful line and would take forty bags at a time when it was established, and would rather hear from the seed house than from the index. **The line is the March's, the March's holding is the trust's, the trust is a named party to a charter condition, and the cost of the refusal is a lost sale of forty-five bags at the trust's price** | Ch 65 |
| **Tova Reed's eighth line, and the lateness** | **the seventh line against the trust's interest becomes the eighth, and it is the first line she has ever entered that says she was wrong about a thing and not about a number. I refused the index to keep the lines unowned. The index has answered by marking one line doubtful. A buyer doing arithmetic will not buy a doubtful line. That is not an attack. It is a buyer doing arithmetic, and I was wrong about the cost.** The number of bags that were not sold was written in pencil and struck out, and Dunmar Fitch made her initial the striking. Entered above it: **four days of lateness on a notice this holding was never served with, entered in her own hand and not softened.** It goes on the Thursday post of the **twenty-second** | Ch 65 |
| **The word *attack*, refused on the page** | **the yard wanted it — the man of about fifty first and loudly, Ilsa Kern second and quietly with a bar in her hand. Dunmar Fitch would not let it stand and made Marek put the proposal and the refusal on the page together with Marek's name against the proposal: a buyer who reads that a name is not established and does not buy is doing what Osvalde Bream did on the fourth of last month when she said she could not tell whether three names were wrong; a district that prints a category cannot be caught at anything, because the category is true; a printer who prints a doubt cannot be caught at anything, because the doubt is true; the oat is doubtful and this holding has said so itself for eleven years in two books, and if a doubt is a wrong then eleven years of this holding is a wrong** | Ch 65 |
| **The seed house's answer, four lines, in Lissa Vale's hand** | **the seed house does not know; it has never known; it copied its own name off a Crown list in a year it does not name; that is the whole of what it knows.** Entered against the ledger and not in it, with the hour, witnessed, and put in the middle of the table. Nobody asked her to lengthen it, nobody asked her which year, and nobody asked for the list, because the Crown printed four hundred copies with the oat in the wrong column once already | Ch 65 |
| **The vote of the twentieth, and its terms** | **every entry from that day forward is read aloud before it is initialled; the reader is a person and not a room; a person is anybody who comes.** Taken standing in daylight with the book shut under Dunmar Fitch's hands. **Five hands from the households present; two of the five had not voted on the fourth, two months back, and one of them said so out loud before putting a hand up. The two strangers on the road did not vote, and Dunmar entered that they did not vote, because a book anybody might read has to show who decided and who only heard.** It is the first thing put to a vote since the fourth, two months back | Ch 66 |
| **Why the vote was not a repeal, said on the page** | **a body cannot consent to the removal of its own record, because the removal is the consent. That is clause two on the fourth sheet, put there to protect the book against the district and now protecting it against us, and it works perfectly.** Anneke Roos, who initialled it against a sentence about the book being evidence, asked for it read back that the sentence she initialled has turned out to be a sentence about the book being a thing that cannot be got back | Ch 66 |
| **Dunmar Fitch's sentence, and the two strangers** | *a book that anybody may read is worth exactly what a reader is prepared to do about it, and this holding has spent a season building the first and has not found the second.* **The reading was interrupted twice by men who had stopped on the road: one made Dunmar say out loud, to a stranger, that he had looked for nine days for a reason for the third levy coming back at the same sum with the same fourteen names and had not found one, and said *that is the first time anybody has said that to me*; the other said there is a name on the notes, the engineer's, that he had heard before and had not heard one of them say out loud, and Dunmar said *there is not going to be*. Neither was entered. A thing said by a stranger on a road is not a record of anything until somebody has heard it twice** | Ch 66 |
| **Tova Reed's door** | **the seed annex is a condition of the charter and the Dryfoot Trust is a named party, and its fourth term sends a copy of everything to Cray; every entry this holding makes is a thing the trust is entitled to have; so the vote of the fourth, two months back, is a vote about who may stand in a yard and not about who may hold a copy. She is not going to go to Cray to warn them, because a steward who warns people about the size of their own rights is a steward who has decided the rights are too big.** Entered by Anneke Roos as an opinion and not as a minute, and Dunmar Fitch did not ask to see it | Ch 66 |
| **The tenth logging of the beat** | **entered on the nineteenth in the same hand, eleven in the quarter hour, cause not established, licensed person present none, and nothing written underneath it by the examiner's decision with her name on the decision. It is read out in public in Chapter 66 on the day it was taken. The beat is not diagnosed and is not going to be** | Ch 66 |
| **The stranger of the twenty-first to the twenty-third** | **a woman of about forty, of a seed house in a town two hundred miles from here with no fen in it, and the house of the buyer who wrote to Cray. A pencil and a notebook and no standing whatsoever, and she says so in the first minute.** She asked for the use log and the standing pressure log to be read, and **Lissa Vale read it herself because she wrote the rule that anybody may ask, and said so out loud: a rule somebody else keeps is a rule that gets kept the way a form gets kept.** She was given the whole of the fen's hydrology, **nothing withheld and nothing softened**, and wrote it in pencil with the notebook open on the table the whole time. She asked the figure eighty a day twice and was told whose it was and **was warned, in advance, what printing a name in a book does to a man under a monitored charter** | Ch 67 |
| **What the stranger said, and it was entered** | **I am going to write to a printer about it. I have to. My house buys on print. If the print is doubtful I need a better print, and I cannot get a better print out of a firm that buys seed.** She then set out in advance what her letter will say, and it is entered with the hour and read back and initialled by her as a true account: that a body in this district keeps a book any stranger may ask to have read, and that a woman in it read the whole of it aloud at a table to a buyer with a pencil, and that everything she read was worse for us than anything we had been told, and that we were four days late with an answer we could have written in a morning. **Nobody in that yard can stop her and nobody in that yard wants to, and she said so out loud so that it would be in the book** | Ch 67 |
| **The two things a reader did that the fen had not seen before** | **she asked what the closing sentence of the margin notes meant, and worked out for herself that the notes that lack it are the sixth and the eighth and that both of those say underneath that they were records and not arguments; and she said out loud that whoever wrote the ninth note was arguing with himself in public, and that she had never in twenty-two years of buying seed seen a book do that, and that she was going to think about the ninth note and was not going to put it in her letter.** Lissa Vale did not enter it, on the grounds that a reader declining to use a thing is not a record of anything except the reader | Ch 67 |
| **The answer to the fieldbook's book** | **asked on the twenty-third, on her way out, by the only stranger in six weeks who had asked it: is there a use log for the fieldbook. Lissa Vale said no. The stranger wrote it down.** **There is no book in this holding that records who has held the instrument, when, or why, and there never has been, and no later chapter may supply one** | Ch 67 |
| **Being readable is a position** | *a woman came two hundred miles to buy an answer about a bean and we handed her a season's water. Being readable is a position and not a virtue. No one asked us to be a good citizen. We are not thanked for it and cannot be.* | Ch 67 |
| **The seal, the order and the map** | **the officer closed the file, all but the string, and said: the district will want the map next, because a Crown seed-route map of this district is nine years old and there is a legend mark on it that this holding can now name. Nobody in that room said the word.** Tova Reed did not touch her case. Marek did not look at the sheet, which has lain on the middle of the table since the twenty-ninth of last month under a reader's own marginal line in her own hand | Ch 68 |

## The section-nine margin notes: the ninth, the tenth and the eleventh, and the counting

**The chain is now eleven and the counting has been checked twice.** Six at the end of Volume 01 → **seven** in Ch 50 (the twenty-third) → **eight** in Ch 58 (the first) → **nine** in Ch 62 (the tenth) → **ten** in Ch 66 (the twentieth) → **eleven** in Ch 68 (the twenty-fourth). All three new notes are signed by nobody but Marek and are read back before they are initialled.

1. **The ninth, Ch 62, the tenth.** Asked on the tenth in front of the district's own man to walk another body's survey and refused for standing and not for right; a copy of that survey offered and not taken; the low quarter keeping its own map at its own cost and this holding keeping its hands off it; the report of the animal of the nineteenth standing in three places with nothing under it; the cost entered, that the fen loses its only leverage where forty-one houses drink off a bed it killed. Closes with the sentence.
2. **The tenth, Ch 66, the twentieth.** The whole of the book read aloud once in daylight on the nineteenth to five households and two strangers, the standing log and the use log and the annex and the beat and the leak and the shortfall and the nine earlier notes and the certified copy and the copy to Cray all in one hearing; the sentence that a readable book is worth what a reader will do about it; the vote that every entry be read aloud by a person and not a room. Closes with the sentence.
3. **The eleventh, Ch 68, the twenty-fourth.** The district officer, the figure, the breach, the district file, the order for the load test, and the map. Closes with the sentence, **and the sentence is on it because the note carries the map question and the map question is an argument** — which is the reason the eighth did not carry it and the reason the tenth does, and Dunmar Fitch writes both of those reasons under the tenth note so that a chain cannot be seen to drop a link.

**The closing sentence, *This is worth nothing against a cut*, stands on nine of the eleven notes and is off the sixth and the eighth — that is, at the moment Chapter 68's note is written, it has been on the last three in a row, and the two that lack it are the only two that lack it.** That is the whole of the pattern and it is stated on the page twice in this batch: **the two notes without it are the two that were records and not arguments, and both of them say so underneath in the engineer's own hand.** The count was verified against Chapter 67's conversation, where a reader of the book works it out for herself from the page. **A later writer may not add a note that carries the sentence without saying whether it is a record or an argument, and may not remove one.**

## The Volume 02 Batch 0002 review pass

**An independent review ran after Chapters 60–68 were drafted and every finding was applied in the prose. The findings are `reviews/volume-02-batch-0002.findings.md` — twenty-two, seven blocking, plus four found while applying them — and this is the state-side record of what a later writer has to know, not a transcript of the prose work.**

**The batch was not restarted. No scene was replaced. No planned plot was changed.** Three things follow from that and are the whole of what changed at the state level:

1. **The batch is days 65–84, the fifth through the twenty-fourth. Day 85 is unused and belongs to Chapters 69–76.** The prompt's ordinal column was off by one against its own weekday column; the weekday column is right, because day 61 is locked as the first and a Thursday, and the chapters were dated from it. The one figure that settles it is Ch 68's own arithmetic: *twenty-three days from the first to the twenty-fourth*, *eleven days since the request was minuted*, *thirty-five days* to the expiry are all true only if the twenty-fourth is day 84.
2. **Kellan Rusk is licensed on the Tarrow water record under a monitored charter, and is not on the rootwright roll.** The three-name rootwright roll is Coll Renner's. The batch had blurred the two, and the blur was load-bearing, because the one Fieldbook panel requires a licensed man. **The two rolls are now distinguished on the page and a later writer may not merge them back.**
3. **The use log is six lines from Chapter 63 and is read as six in Ch 63, Ch 66 and Ch 67.** The table is above. **Line six records only the contact and the hour, so the panel remains quoted in no document after the fix.**

**The dating pass, in one line: sixteen uses of *two months back* were applied to days 31–60, which are *last month*, and all sixteen are corrected. Three uses survive and all three are genuine October.** One instruction in the review to change an eleventh from *two months back* to *last month* was **wrong** — the eleventh follows the tenth in the same month, and that month is October — and was not applied.

**The arithmetic pass, in one line:** the lateness figures now run four on the eighteenth, six on the twentieth, **eight** on the twenty-second, and each is stated on the day it is true; **Cray is nine hundred miles by the March road and a hundred and eleven further by the ordinary cart, and the distance from the seventh to the fourteenth is seven days; the sale was forty-five bags and not forty; the index is a hundred and forty copies of two hundred pages and not a hundred and forty sheets; the three acres went sour three weeks ago and not two months; the price list at eighteen pounds is four levies of four pounds five and not a year's raise; and the purchase of the index is paid for before it arrives, in a hand to a carter on the Friday, not on the Thursday post after it has already been on the table.**

**Prose repaired without changing the plot:** two set-pieces that read as beat lists are broken into paragraphs; Kellan's bench speech is whole in Ch 63 and compressed in Ch 68, where he points at the fen's own hand instead; the buyer is a woman throughout; the collie is out of the public reading; the man of about fifty's non-vote is entered; two of Chapter 60's staging paragraphs and one of Chapter 65's were cut for repetition; the stray double blank line and four *hand flat* gestures are gone; and Chapter 65, which had entered the same line twice in one scene and re-narrated the post's arrival after playing it out, now does each once.

**Known and accepted, and not to be re-attempted: the staging.** Eight of nine chapters sit at the seed-house table. The review proposed moving two of the batch's central set-pieces out of it; **both were declined at the fix pass, because both are long unbroken dialogue and moving either one costs more than it buys, and the residue is recorded here as a weakness rather than fixed silently.** A later writer should vary the staging deliberately and should not assume the batch modelled it.

**Length after the pass: 9 chapters, 2,507–3,100 words, 25,388 total.** Two chapters went over the ceiling when the fixes landed and were trimmed back into it by cutting restatement, not by cutting a scene. **Chapters 60 and 63 are the two nearest a ceiling and a later writer should trim rather than pad.**

## The Fieldbook panel: one spent, in Chapter 63, and the allowance

**Spent, once, in Chapter 63, on the twelfth, at the eleventh hour, at the well house, on the head of the node.** **Kellan Rusk is a licensed man on the Tarrow water record, under a monitored charter. He is not on the rootwright roll and never was: that roll has three names and Coll Renner's license is on it. The two rolls are now distinguished on the page in Ch 63 and a later writer may not blur them back together.** He tested the contact with a plain wire gauge and wrote the reading of the test down before he let the instrument near anything. **Lissa Vale wrote the use-log line first, on the page, and read it back, and Marek initialled it after her.** The instrument came out of the case Lissa Vale had carried down from the seed house that morning; it has been on Marek's mother's table since the twenty-first of last month, and **it is in the instrument and not in a person**, and it is set as a block quotation of three plain lines in the volume's own house format:

> This is the mouth.
>
> There is more than one source and it will not say which.
>
> A node does not keep a list.

**It is spent on a thing the instrument can read.** It is not spent on the seed line, on the bead, on the hand in the register, on the market, or on a page: the instrument reads a node and Chapter 59 has established on the page that it cannot read paper, and this batch did not reopen that.

**It cost something and the cost is on the page.** Eleven seconds he cannot account for, as with the four on the cut face; the four inches on his forearm hot and staying hot; his hand from the wrist down no use for the rest of that day and most of the next; Kellan Rusk holding the case down the well house steps because Marek could not get the latch with two fingers. **And the third line refused the fen the answer it wanted, in the same three lines, which is the cost that matters: the eighty a day is a figure about a mouth, and nothing will name the sources, and the fen's own book cannot hold it.**

**Nothing in this batch quotes the three lines in a document, and the use-log line records only that the instrument was laid on the head at a stated hour with a licensed man present.** Lissa Vale wrote nothing else, and gave the reason in one sentence, and it is the same rule Dunmar Fitch applied on the third of last month to a different set of lines: *there is nothing in it that a man with a rule can check, and a line in a book that cannot be checked is a line somebody can use.* The reading went into the book as **a contact and not as a finding**.

**The Volume 02 allowance is now zero for Batch 0002, and one panel per batch is available to Batches 0003, 0004 and 0005.**

## Stage and contact accounting after Volume 02 Batch 0002

- **Marek is Stage 2, Grafthand, and this batch does not move him.** The stage is one small graft old. He can make a small reliable graft between living tissue and a limited local branch and he can keep two or three active links; a fourth fails. **He still cannot draw, has no bead, no anchor work, no bridge above a local conduit, no seedheart, no caretaker link and no reading of the deep archive, and the touchroot sense is unchanged and still unreliable and still worth nothing in front of anybody except a licensed engineer standing next to him.** No number in this batch may be used to move him.
- **The rootmark is present and unchanged: four inches along the inside of the left forearm, branching twice, permanent, recorded in this holding's book by Coll Renner under his own hand with the hour and the witnesses.** In this batch it is hot after a contact and the hand is useless for two days in four, and **nobody in this yard wrote the word *became* and nobody may.**
- **Contacts: four behind him, unchanged in kind and cost, plus one contact made in Chapter 63 and entered as a contact and not as a finding.** **The Ch 63 contact is not a fifth contact in the Volume 01 sense and may not be counted as one.** It was eleven seconds at a node, with a licensed man, with the contact tested and the use-log line written and read back first, it cost the mark and two days of the hand, and it produced three lines that cannot be checked and cannot be entered.
- **No draw and no return by rootlight in this batch, and the panel's third line is the reason the volume does not need one: a node does not keep a list.**

## New locks from the drafted Volume 02 Batch 0002

- **A refusal is a place, and the place is this yard.** The batch's governing fact. It is entered in the book with the number of houses the refusal reached, which is one, and it may not be softened by any later chapter into anything about markets being wrong.
- **Nobody may call the index's doubt an attack, and the proposal and the refusal are both on the page with Marek's name against the proposal.** A true thing that costs this holding money is not a crime, and a category that is true cannot be caught at anything. This is the same rule that governs the Crown-held column and the legend, and it now governs a private printer as well.
- **A body that is owed a survey may not also be the body that goes and collects it, and may not hold a copy of one either.** The low quarter's map stays in the low quarter's hands and the fen's copy of it stays unopened on the fen's own table. The cost is entered, and the fen has lost the only leverage it had in the district on the one ground where forty-one houses are still drinking off a bed it killed, **and it knew that when it did it.**
- **The open book cannot be repealed, and a body cannot consent to the removal of its own record.** The vote of the twentieth is not a repeal and is not a softening: it is a body that cannot unwrite a page and will not stop writing. **A later batch may not have this body vote the book shut, and may not have the district waive the condition.**
- **Every entry is read aloud before it is initialled, and the reader is a person.** A person is anybody who comes. **This rule is now in force and every subsequent entry in the volume obeys it on the page, and a chapter that enters something without showing the reading is a chapter that has broken it.**
- **Being readable is a position and not a virtue, and the holding is not thanked for it and cannot be.** No one has asked it to be a good citizen. This must not be converted into a compliment in a later chapter.
- **There is no use log for the fieldbook, there never has been, and there is not going to be.** A stranger asked and Lissa Vale said no in one word. **A later chapter may not supply one, may not have anybody propose one, and may not have anybody thank anybody for the absence.**
- **The instrument reads a node and not a book, and the third line of the Ch 63 panel is in force: a node does not keep a list.** The fen cannot manufacture provenance out of the ground. This is the rule Movement 3 will work against, and a later batch may not quietly suspend it.
- **The district is not a villain and did not act in this batch.** Every Crown act is correct, timely and structural: a quarterly article is the trade and not a raid, a return is a document and is produced before a figure is heard, a file is the only place a private draw can be recorded without consent, a load test is ordered because a node with an untestable condition is hard to defend, and the map is wanted because a nine-year-old sheet carries a mark this holding can now name. **A later writer may not let the two be confused, and may not let a district officer lie, obstruct, or act corruptly.**
- **The figure of eighty a day is in the district file and will be read at the next renewal, a variation, or an objection.** The invitation works both ways. This is not a rescue and the fen did not want it.
- **The open book's cost is now paid three ways and no chapter may say it was free: a lost sale of forty-five bags entered in Tova Reed's own hand as the first line she has ever entered that says she was wrong about a thing and not about a number; the fen's only leverage in the district; and the second condition of clause three taken out of the building in a stranger's notebook.**
- **Kellan Rusk's breach stands disclosed in his own words, beside the figure and not below it.** He is not rescued, not forgiven and not thanked. His monitored charter is not renewed, reduced or resolved by this batch. **The word *became* and the word *redeemed* are both prohibited for him in this volume.**
- **Nothing romantic has happened and nothing may lean that way.** Tova Reed put two sheets of yard paper in her own book and did not give them back and said the compost and not the other thing. **That is the whole of it and it is institutional, and no later chapter may make it anything else.**
- **Lissa Vale is not forgiven and is not thanked.** She proposed the two shillings and said so in the book; she read the whole of the use log to a stranger herself and gave the reason out loud; she wrote the use-log line before the instrument; she refused to enter the panel; she wrote a rule in October and told a stranger it was hers to keep; and she answered *no*, in one word, to the only question nobody had asked in six weeks. **Her refusal of the fourth of last month is hers, is not Sera Quill's, is not her own fourth, and is not numbered.**
- **Nia is not in this batch.** Not in a room, not in a letter, not by report, and nobody may say she decided not to deliver the letter. **The letter is in the open drawer of the seed-house table, sealed.** The absence is a working absence: the monthly report, the mill figures, the reading aloud, the rule about the reader being a person and the answer to a stranger all got done by other people in three weeks, and the fen has not once written down why.
- **Sera Quill is not in this batch, is not asked about Brinewake, and nobody has asked about her fourth refusal.** It is unnumbered and it may not be numbered. **The next monthly examination, at which the load test is to be performed with a licensed engineer present, is the one at which the volume's first licensed witness will have to be somebody else**, and no chapter may have her simply arrive.
- **Sera Quill, the fieldbook, the C/4, the register, the long line, the bypass, the Thornwild settlement, the word *seat*, Marda Vole, Dunmar's *none known*, the sealed envelope, and the works schedule behind the certified page are all untouched by this batch and none of them may be undone by it.** The bypass is unbuilt, unfunded, and Ivo Tarrant's four men do not come back. The register's log is still not produced and the applicant is not entitled to it. **No second demand for the register may be sent and none was.**
- **The leak is still a leak and is still not mended: five-eighths of an inch, twice measured, two rules, at the seventh hour, pump on, into the housing and not into the pit, the pit four feet of iron water and not up a quarter of an inch.** One sixteenth in thirteen days and still no rate written. **It cannot be mended under load and the panel did not diagnose it and nothing in this batch made it a mystery.** The beat is logged ten times and undiagnosed. The 399 hundredweight shortfall is carried forward and not discharged and is seventy-nine pounds sixteen at four shillings. The compost line is still nothing put in, none available, last put in twenty-one hundredweight and a quarter on the fourteenth by levy. The bed is at three and a half degrees. The graft is being fed about a twentieth of what it was designed to be fed and the starve is still not visible from the top of the field.

## Proposed canon changes recorded, not silently applied — Volume 02 Batch 0002

Batch 0002 added eight things by drafting. All are local detail, mechanisms, forms, prices, places or people, and none changes a world rule:

1. **The Ashcombe index as a purchased object** — two hundred pages, two shillings, a subscription rather than a purchase, and a copy on the middle of the fen's own table beside the fieldbook and the use log. **A body that buys its enemy's printer and puts it in the room with its own record is a new posture and it is now established.**
2. **The trade article and the professional quarterly** — the body is the same body Pike's circular went out in, the article carries no name and no license number and is technically accurate, and the defence anybody has is a book. **A market in methods, and the first appearance of a Crown-facing trade that is not a raid.**
3. **Form RW/3, the schedule of works** — one page, a box for the licensed engineer, a box for the four conditions, a blank line at the foot for the order of the work. **A new form class, and the shape of the method as a thing a foreman can carry to a holding he has never seen.** The order of the work is refused and the compost is the reason.
4. **Renner's price list and route** — day work at four shillings, a yard at a pound a month, a copper collar at two shillings, a crew of four for a season at eighteen pounds, eleven farms on the fen road. **A price list is a new document class in the volume and it is the first material anybody has put on that table in a season.**
5. **The C/4's date as the fen's own computation** — the fen is not the addressee, is not entitled to a copy of the notice, does not know when it was served, and enters the fourteenth as a computation and not as a fact. **A clock the holding cannot check, and a standing lateness that runs from the fourteenth of this month.**
6. **The vote of the twentieth** — every entry read aloud before it is initialled, the reader is a person, a person is anybody who comes, and the fact of who voted and who only heard is entered. **A new standing practice inside an existing charter condition, adopted by the body and not by the district.**
7. **The stranger of the twenty-first to the twenty-third** — a seed buyer with a pencil, a town two hundred miles off with no fen in it, and the disclosure that she is going to write to a printer. **A seed house outside the district is now a party to this holding's record without ever having been one, and the mechanism is the annex's fourth term.**
8. **Hilary Skeath, of the district water office for the Tarrow district**, about thirty-five, who gives her name once at a gate and is entered in the book by the clerk, and who orders the full-draw load test and says the district will want the map next. **A named district water officer for the winter.** She is **not** a member of the cooperative, and **no new named member of the cooperative was created in this batch**: the crew is still Ilsa Kern, Jonas Byre, Anneke Roos, Gael Senn, Lissa Vale and Dunmar Fitch, plus the man of about fifty and the man of about seventy, who are held by function and may not be given names without recording it.

None of the eight changes a world rule, a power rule, a relationship lock, or the ending. **The ending is untouched: Chancellor Iona Vey remains the final antagonist, the Rootway is not a mind, and the volume's question — who altered the maps and the seed records enough to make a bridge between this fen and the dry Salt March legal, and who was paid for the alteration — is still unanswered and has gained one line, not one answer.** The ninth lock is the district's statement that it will want the map next, and nobody in that room said the word.

# VOLUME 02 — BATCH 0003, CHAPTERS 69–76, MOVEMENT 3

**Volume 02, *The Graft Market*. Eight chapters, Movement 3, *The Memory In The Seed*. The volume's outline is `outline/volume-02.md`. Its governing prompt is `workspace/volume-02/batch-0003/PROMPT.md`, which was written from the outline and the state files in the same phase that drafted the batch, because Movement 3 had no card sheet. Volume 01 is closed. Volume 02 Batches 0001 and 0002 are closed. Nothing below reopens any of them.**

Ch 69 days 85–87 (**Sun the twenty-fifth, Mon the twenty-sixth, Tue the twenty-seventh**) · Ch 70 days 88–90 (**Wed the twenty-eighth, Thu the twenty-ninth, Fri the thirtieth**) · Ch 71 day 91 (**Sat the first of the next month**) · Ch 72 days 93–94 (**Mon the third, Tue the fourth**) · Ch 73 days 95–96 (**Wed the fifth, Thu the sixth**) · Ch 74 days 97–99 (**Fri the seventh, Sat the eighth, Sun the ninth**) · Ch 75 days 100–101 (**Mon the tenth, Tue the eleventh**) · Ch 76 days 102–104 (**Wed the twelfth, Thu the thirteenth, Fri the fourteenth**).

**The batch runs days 85–104, the twenty-fifth of one month through the fourteenth of the next. Day 92, the second of the next month, falls inside Ch 72's span and is the night the trays come down; it carries no chapter of its own and no entry of its own in the fen's books.** Every weekday follows from **day 61 = the first = a Thursday**, locked in Volume 01 Chapter 49. The three weekday statements on the page that a reviewer may check the chain against are **the full-draw load test held on a Saturday** (Ch 71, the first), **the vote taken on a Friday** (Ch 74, the seventh), and **Coll Renner coming up the fen road on a Monday** (Ch 75, the tenth).

Day 119 is **the twenty-nine** — the twenty-ninth of the month after next, the expiry. Every distance in these eight chapters was computed from the day numbers:
