**No distance in the batch's own table was got wrong and the chain above is the one on the page.** The one figure that could have been wrong and was checked twice is the count of the readings of the seventh column, which is a running count across four chapters and reconciles exactly: **ten on the twenty-sixth (Ch 82), sixteen on the second (Ch 85), seventeen on the third (Ch 86) when it was filled.** The days 107–116 are ten; 107–122 are sixteen; 107–123 are seventeen. All three are right.

### The March's tin is *day minus nineteen*, and the rule is unbroken

day 105 = 86 · 119 = **100** · 121 = 102 · 123 = 104 · 125 = 106 · 129 = **110**. The tin is unmoved throughout, on a bed at three and a half degrees, with both undertakings standing — the seed house's that it will not be moved and the trust's that it will not be asked. **The dry-land line is no longer recorded as lost, and a cutting of it is in a tray on this fen four inches high, sown before the sowing closed on the twenty-ninth.**

## Numbers and records established in Chapters 77–88

A later writer must not change these without recording the change. Every figure below is additive. **No figure established in Volume 01 or in Volume 02 Batches 0001, 0002 or 0003 was altered.**

| Fact | Value | Established |
|---|---|---|
| **The compost bay** | **eleven feet by seven, beaten earth and boards, a roof of oiled sacking on four posts, at the north end of the seed house yard, built by Jonas Byre and the man of about fifty in the third week of a month two months back because Anneke Roos said a body ought to have a place to put the thing that comes back down a route.** Two boards on its north wall: the big one with RETURN cut in Jonas Byre's hand, and a second, smaller one, cut by Ilsa Kern on the eighteenth of a month two months back and not nailed to anything. **The number of days the bay has stood empty is cut under both: 39 on the fifteenth, 41 on the twenty-fourth, 58 on the fourth of next month.** It is empty throughout and the number is still going up | Ch 77, 81, 82, 86 |
| **The seed-route schedule** | **the first public schedule of a route in this fen, ruled by hand by Dunmar Fitch on the seventeenth on the seed house's own account paper, in Tova Reed's hand, with no number on it and none anywhere, on Anneke Roos's standing that a form with a number is a form somebody can make a class of and a piece of paper with rules on it can be copied by anybody who has seen it.** Title: *Seed-route schedule, from the seed house gate at Lantern Reach to the far end. One book. Kept on the well house door. Every entry read aloud before it is initialled. The reader is a person. A person is anybody who comes.* **Seven columns: the day; the crate, numbered; the lot and the keeper of it, in the keeper's own words; the weight; the first end, the hour it left and a signature; the second end, the hour it arrived and a signature; the return.** A route line at the top of the sheet: *first end, the first break, the low dry, the second break, and beyond.* Nailed on the well house door on the seventeenth with four nails. It is still on the door. **A copy at the second end, nailed to a post inside a thorn and hazel fence on the low dry, unsigned** | Ch 78, 82, 87 |
| **The two end columns and the return column** | **the second end's column has carried *eleven households, keeper not named* since the seventeenth of a month two months back, before anybody had been, and it was never changed and nobody ever put a name in it.** The sixth column at the far end is empty and stays empty: a settlement with no charter has signed nothing and has been asked to sign nothing. The seventh column, the return, was **read empty ten times by the twenty-sixth, sixteen times by the second of next month, and filled on the third** | Ch 78, 82, 85, 86, 87, 88 |
| **The declared capacity** | **thirty crates a month out and thirty a month back, being a cart a day on the working days of a month, and the return is entered in the seventh column and is not part of this figure.** Entered on the twenty-fifth of a month two months back at the ninth hour, read aloud by the man of about fifty, and true when it was written | Ch 82 |
| **The measured capacity** | **six crates out in a month and none back. Six is a fifth of thirty.** Entered on the twenty-sixth on the district's own discipline — the load entered before the reading — in the yard, in daylight, at the well house door, read aloud by the man of about fifty, and the smaller figure is on the same sheet in the same hand under the larger one, **and the seven items that produced it are entered with it**: one loaded cart over the first break in a week and no loaded cart back; fourteen shillings a day for a cart and two men; a levy at four times is eighteen pounds and came in at thirteen pounds four; the seventh column is empty; the bed is at three and a half degrees with nothing going into the return main for two months; the return main is twenty-two rods of eight inch against forty-one of twelve; and there is one cart available in this holding, borrowed, for one day a month | Ch 82 |
| **Coll Renner's capacity sentence, and where it is and is not** | **in the fen's book with the hour on it, as his and not as a finding, and NOT on the form, and the not-on-the-form is entered as a downgrade because a licensed man's professional opinion in a book anybody may copy has no form in this district either. He said it at a gate, and a gate is not a document.** *A local conduit takes water from one place to another place. A seed-route bridge takes seed from one keeper to another keeper, and it has two ends and a return, and the return is the part nobody ever wants to pay for.* And: *you do not name a bridge for the distance, you name it for the load, and a man who has written down the miles has not decided what the thing is for* | Ch 77, 78 |
| **A bridge with no return is a road with crates on it** | **Marek Vale's sentence, in the yard at dusk on the twenty-fourth, and Jonas Byre's answer to it: write that down, because I did not say it and you did, and I would rather it was in your mouth than mine** | Ch 81, 86 |
| **The route and its distances** | **eleven miles of fen road, then four bad miles of March road, then the first break, then the low dry, then the second break.** The first break is **a mile short of the low dry and about forty yards long**, peat gone out of the top on both sides, sides slumped, standing water to the depth of a boot, gravel that has had a foot of water stood on it, no stone within a mile and none within three of the low dry. The second break is **a quarter mile past the low dry: running sand with a soft rise at the far end.** **The road holds at the first break and does not hold at the second, and the route therefore ends at the low dry and does not go on to the March road — and that is the first fact about a seed-route bridge anybody in this fen has ever written down** | Ch 81, 87, 88 |
| **The carters' man, and the two sentences that fixed the batch** | **about sixty, nineteen years crossing bad ground at the time of the walk and thirty-one years in the trade: you can go over loaded and you cannot come back loaded, ever, not this month and not in the spring — a loaded cart presses the water down and it comes up on the far side, and a loaded cart coming back onto it puts the wheels in the hole it made on the way out. And he will not put a second loaded cart over in the same place inside a week, because the place he has just used is a hole now.** A second carters' man, about sixty, thirty-one years, said why he stood in a yard all Saturday for five shillings: *because you paid for me to be here, and if I go home I have not been here, and I would like to be a man you remember that way* | Ch 81, 87 |
| **The carts, the levy, and the road money** | **two carts at five shillings a day each and two carters' men at two shillings a day, fourteen shillings a day, wanted on the morning. Plank and chain at the crossing, two. The whole of the twenty-fourth was sixteen shillings and nothing moved; the whole of the sixth of next month was sixteen shillings and something did.** The seed house tin held **thirteen shillings and a shilling** on the fifteenth and a day's carts came to fourteen. **The levy was put at four times what it was — eighteen pounds, being four times four pounds five — and Jonas Byre had said on the fifth of a month two months back that it would be, and it did, and Anneke Roos asked for both halves in one paragraph. Thirteen pounds four came in; four pounds sixteen did not, and is entered as not received, not remitted, and not carried as a debt against anybody, because a body cannot make a debt of a man who has chosen not to pay it and then call it a debt in its own book without becoming something else** | Ch 81, 87 |
| **Wil Tam, mender of implements** | **keeps a bothy at the crossing, is the man the carters go to, mended a chain with a cold rivet for two shillings, and would not take anything at all for a split tire and said why: you have not had a tire off me before and I would like there to be a first one that cost you nothing, and next one is two and I will tell you so at the time.** He charged the three of them two shillings for the floor at the bothy and would not charge for the fire, and asked whether the second break had been walked, and was told it had, and said that a man who has walked the second break and come back is a man who has been somewhere most men in this trade have only heard of | Ch 81, 83 |
| **The terms of the route** | **six clauses, drafted by the engineer of record on the twenty-first alone, and a good draft.** One: what is carried, crate by crate, with the number, the lot and its keeper in the keeper's words, the weight, the hour out, the hour in, and a signature at each end. **Two: a copy of the record is kept at each end and each end answers on it within the month — STRUCK OUT by the steward of the trust on the twenty-second, in the yard, in daylight, in one line, not scratched.** Three: the return is entered in the seventh column, and where that column is empty the route goes and nothing comes back and neither end has to excuse it. Four: one book, not kept in two places, and either end may ask to have any entry read aloud, the reader is a person, a person is anybody who comes, and the reading is before the initial and not after. Five: either end may stop the route on a day's notice by writing it in the book, no reason required, none to be entered. Six: a season, and at the end of it the route goes on by a new writing or stops, and stopping needs no reason, no paper and no notice, **and a route that stops is not a route that has failed.** The terms are in this holding's book and in no other book in the world, and are held by nobody at the far end | Ch 80 |
| **Clause five is the good one** | **Anneke Roos found nothing to fault in five of the six and said so in a room with the engineer of record sitting at it, and said she wanted it entered that she had been looking for a fault in all six and had not found one and was suspicious of a document that cannot be faulted. And that clause five was the best sentence anybody had written in this building in eleven weeks, and that she would be annoyed if she was thanked for it** | Ch 80 |
| **Clause four is the price of the document** | **Marek's own finding, entered: clause four is the clause he cares about most and the one he would not give up, and it is also the clause that puts her in a document exactly as much as clause two does, and clause two was the attempt not to pay it. Tova Reed's answer: I would rather be examinable than quiet. Neither of them can answer it for the other and both of them are right** | Ch 80 |
| **The fen's answer about the trade's own words** | **a body with no charter can be a party to nothing, and that is what a charter is, and it is the only thing it is; and a settlement with no charter cannot be a party to anything either; so the two ends of this route are the two ends in this district that can be party to each other at all, and there is no form that says so and nobody is going to write one.** And the plain version, said at the gate by the man of about fifty and **not entered anywhere and never was**: *we are not on anything now, and there is nothing over there that says what they are, and so we can be with them without it being a thing that is on a form anywhere* | Ch 84 |
| **Wrenlow** | **eleven plots in a ring about two hundred yards across, on the low dry, a well at the low end and in, two rooms and a stove and a table, and a fence of thorn and hazel round the whole of it that a body has cut and re-cut for as long as anybody there can remember. Eleven households live in the two rooms and in four of the plots. Each household has a plot of about a sixteenth of an acre and the plots are not good ground. There is no gate because there has never been anything at Wrenlow worth closing.** The fen's own second-end line has read *eleven households, keeper not named* since before anybody had been | Ch 83, 87 |
| **Wenna Roke** | **thirty-nine, keeps seed for the eleven households, uses the fen's own words in a yard without having been told them and the fen never found out whether she had read them or been told.** She keeps a short line from under the north embankment nine miles east alive in all eleven plots for two winters, because a line in one plot dies with a bad month. **Her list is one sheet of lining paper with a stone on it, eleven lines, one to each household, with what the household will sow and what it had last season, and no column for where any of it came from, because eleven households have brought her a sack out of a cart in nine years through a man who wanted to be helpful and not one of them said where it came from, and she did not ask, because on the day she asks the sacks stop coming.** She can read numbers and the words she uses and nothing else, and she has never been handed a form in her life. She is not a member of anything and is not of the fen's body | Ch 83, 87 |
| **The two refusals at the far end** | **Both entered as the fact of a refusal and not its terms, with a line in the engineer's hand under the first: this entry records the fact of a refusal and not its terms, and the terms are not held by this body.** The first, concerning the record: she will not keep a copy and will not answer on it, *because I have never signed a paper in my life, and if I sign that one I am in it, and if I am in it then the day I want out of it there is no day.* The second, concerning a return: **everything coming back down this road goes to Cray, and a thing that goes to Cray does not stop here on the way, so I am the end of a road, and the road is for somebody else, and I am not going to be the first thing in a column to make a fen's door look right.** Her condition: leave my name off it, and whoever is standing here when it comes can put a mark on it | Ch 83, 86, 88 |
| **The terms were sent and returned** | **four sheets, copied at Fennmere, the strike on clause two copied as a strike, given to Jonas Byre at the gate at the eighth hour on the twenty-seventh, unasked for, by Tova Reed, who knew better. Returned into the yard on the twenty-eighth in the inside pocket of a coat, folded twice, with two sentences the carrier was given and entered in his words with the hour: don't send me anything with my name at the top of it, and if you want to keep a thing going, come back with the thing and not with a paper.** Entered by the steward against her own interest as a fault and not as a piece of good practice, and entered by Anneke Roos as a fault of her own, that she had been asked to check the paper before it went and had not been | Ch 83 |
| **The convoy** | **twelve crates, numbered one to twelve, on the fifth of next month, written down crate by crate. One to six: the seed house's own bean of Lantern Reach, out of the seed house's own saving, lot of the season before last, from the low strip above the toe ditch, about eleven pounds a crate. Seven to twelve: the March's line, Dryfoot Trust, out of the tin, sown in this seed house before the sowing closed, the eleventh of eleven cuttings, about twenty-nine pounds a crate, in six trays in soil under four inches of green.** The third column carries the keeper's own words, and for seven to twelve those words are *the March's line*, and **a line is not a lot, and Tova Reed refuses to correct it because the column is the keeper's words.** The list is in date order, ruled before anybody knew what would be on it, **and the fen did not reorder it** | Ch 87, 88 |
| **The wrong half** | **six of the twelve crates travelled and six did not, and the six that travelled were the fen's own bean because it was ready when the list was ruled, and the six that stayed are the only four inches of the March's dry line in this district.** The reason, in Tova Reed's words and Marek's together: a body that has spent a season refusing to choose for other people does not get to choose which line reaches a settlement because of the date on a list; the road chose, the ground chose, the list chose, and the list is a piece of paper. **She said she would have reordered it, and nobody argued with her and nobody said she was wrong, and the knowing is entered and the knowing is not in capitals** | Ch 87, 88 |
| **The certificate of forty-one words** | **on lining paper with no printed head, in Marek Vale's own hand, and the only certificate in this batch anybody asked for: *Six crates. The seed house's own bean of Lantern Reach. The ground is this fen, the low strip above the toe ditch. The name the seed house has printed for this bean was copied off a Crown list in a year it does not name, and it does not know where the bean came from. Eleven years kept. It will do on poor dry ground. Weighed at this end at the gate.*** He read it to her and then said it again without the paper. **She could not read it, and said so, and would not pretend she had.** Her answer: *then take the name off the tin, not off the book, and write on the tin what it is, because a thing in a sack with the true name on it is worth more to me than a sack with a good name on it that a woman nine miles away has spent eleven years not being able to check.* She kept the paper with a stone on it, and it is the first thing a settlement with no charter has ever been asked to keep | Ch 87, 88 |
| **The return that is not compost** | **entered in the seventh column on the third of next month: the March's dry line sown in this fen's ground, to be lifted in the autumn, the seed off it the March's line grown in this fen, and it goes back down the March road to Cray in a crate with a sheet on it, weighed at this end and at that end and signed by a person at each end. It is not a hundredweight of compost and it is not money. And the whole of it goes to Cray and does not stop at the second end, and the second end's column is empty and stays empty this season** | Ch 86, 88 |
| **The expiry** | **at midnight on the twenty-ninth, on a date nobody chose, in the ordinary way, with no appeal anywhere in the four clauses. No officer obstructed anything, no officer had to be there, nothing anybody did that day could have kept it alive and nothing anybody did that day had killed it.** Nine items were read as nine items; **the use log was found on the morning of the vote to have never been a charter condition at all**, and had been kept as one since the tenth of a month four months back, and Lissa Vale had known and had not said; **the return main to specification was withdrawn from the motion as a thing this body cannot promise and entered as a fact, twenty-two rods of eight inch, and nobody will ever come and look at it again, and that is now correct, and it is not a defeat**; item nine, the third of the three conditions in clause three, was not voted on and stays blank | Ch 84 |
| **The vote of the twenty-ninth** | **seven hands, four for, three against, taken standing in daylight in the yard with the book shut under the clerk's hands and the five sheet open on a chair.** For: Tova Reed (one sentence about the copy to Cray going out every time with nothing behind it but a signature), Lissa Vale (no reason given, the first time in a season, and none required), Anneke Roos (a body that cannot consent to the removal of its own record might as well keep it), Dunmar Fitch (the only item he can be responsible for, because it is the only one that is a sentence). **Against: Marek Vale, the engineer of record, who put his own name against his own vote for the second time in a season** — the book was worth the trouble because somebody was going to make us produce it, and there is nobody going to make us produce it now, and he would rather be found out in a year than have it found out in five; **Jonas Byre**, against the sixth item, that a copy of every entry goes to another body's hands for eleven years with no charter and no box at the far end that can be filled in; **and the man of about fifty**, on the worst reason in the batch, that a body which is proud of being honest has stopped asking the question, and how a body is known is not how it is honest, it is how big it is, and he would rather this fen was small and did not know for one more season whether it can be, and he asked for it in the book as a bad reason with his name on it and not argued with. **The five households who came to the gate, the woman with the shilling, and the man of about seventy, who was at his sister's and had not been asked to come, did not vote in either direction, and the clerk entered that** | Ch 84 |
| **The third of the three conditions in clause three, after the expiry** | **NOT DESCRIBED, NOT NAMED, NOT GUESSED AT, and now untested and unowned.** The examination at which it is tested is the first examination held after the term ends; that was the seventh hour of the first of next month; the examination was held; **the condition was not described, because there was no officer of the district present and none was required to be, because there is no form, because there is nothing to appoint an examiner under, and because there is no charter.** The fen did not write and ask, and entered the reason — a second form produces nothing on purpose, because a body that asks twice has told a district office that asking works — and Marek's own line, that he would rather it were a hole we knew about, and the clerk's answer, that a body which enters what it is afraid of has started keeping records for somebody else. **It will be described at an examination, on a form, to whoever holds the node on that day, and nobody in Tarrow Fen has any idea what it is.** This is now the single hardest thing in the volume and it is a form, and the form's date has been and gone | Ch 84, 85, 88 |
| **The district's notice of the second** | **four lines on the office's paper, and the language of the eighteenth of a month four months back again, fifteen weeks on, with not one word of it changed: the node at Lantern Reach is not shown to be maintained, and the district may at any time make arrangements for its maintenance, and may do so without notice to any person not in occupation of the ground.** Nailed on the well house door **on the left of the schedule**, on the man of about seventy's standing, and it is correct and nobody in the yard was a villain about it. The pump kept running, nothing was sealed, the head was not touched, **and the fen did not use the expiry to stop measuring the leak** | Ch 85 |
| **The district's answer, of the ninth** | **four days over four weeks after the return of the fourteenth of last month — which the fen answered on the twenty-first of last month, and which the district received a schedule against on the twenty-fifth of last month — four paragraphs and a clerk's signature, and it is correct.** One: the seed-route sheet is not a survey and says so on its own face, is not amended on the statement of a holder, is revised on a cycle, and **this office does not fix the cycle and cannot say what it is.** Two: the district enters this holding as a local holder of an unmapped root line for the purposes of a schedule check, in the district's book and not in the Crown's map layer, **and the applicant is not entitled to the map layer and has not asked for it.** Three: **a route carrying seed over ground the district carries in schedule is a use of that ground; a use is a class of use; classes of use carry terms; those terms are not settled by this office; they will be sent in the ordinary course and will name a rate and a class of use, and a copy will go to the holder of the node and to any person who has written to this office about the ground.** Four: the schedule received is received, and the office has no power to approve it, no power to stop it, and **no duty to send any person to hear it read.** And: **this letter asks for no fee and says nothing about whether a fee will be asked for.** **It is not an answer, not an exemption and not a tax, and it opens Movement 5** | Ch 88 |
| **Ottoline Fray's cutting sheet** | **four lines and a sheet, off her own book, the first cutting sheet she has ever written and eleven weeks old when she sent it: she has put in the third column what she has been told the third column is for and she wants to know whether she has got it right, and a man at Fennmere has told her there is no column for it and that this is a fault of her trade and not of her paper, and she has decided the fault of her trade can be put on her paper in her own hand, and she would rather be corrected in a book than corrected nowhere, and she is not going to be helped and she is not going to be refused.** Entered as received on the fourth of next month, read aloud twice, **and unanswered, and the fen entered that nobody had answered it and that a body with no charter and no standing has no method for a thing like this** | Ch 86 |
| **The reading rule as a practice** | **every entry is read aloud before it is initialled, the reader is a person, and a person is anybody who comes — and in this batch that rule is what makes a public schedule possible, and it is carried by persons the reader cannot read, and the reader is always a named person and the man of about fifty reads a great deal of it in this batch** | Ch 77, 78, 82, 84, 86, 88 |
| **Lissa Vale's rule, said out loud for the third time** | **there is nothing in that a man with a rule cannot look at twice and get the same answer, and she is not going to add anything to the return column's entry, and she wants that written down as the reason she added nothing.** The first two times were in Batch 0002 and Batch 0003, on a panel and on a reading | Ch 86 |

## The section-nine margin notes: the fourteenth to the seventeenth, and the counting

**The chain is now seventeen and the counting has been checked.** Thirteen at the end of Batch 0003 → **fourteen** in Ch 78 (the eighteenth) → **fifteen** in Ch 81 (the twenty-fourth) → **sixteen** in Ch 84 (the twenty-ninth) → **seventeen** in Ch 88 (the ninth). All four are signed by nobody but Marek and all four are read back before they are initialled.

1. **The fourteenth, Ch 78, the eighteenth.** A form ruled by hand with seven columns and no number on it, nailed on the well house door on the seventeenth; a return column read empty with the number of the readings kept under it; a licensed man's sentence entered as his and not put on the form; an objection by a member of the body that a column which has never been filled is not a broken promise but a line anybody may write on; a board over a bay; and eleven days. **An argument. Closes with the sentence.**
2. **The fifteenth, Ch 81, the twenty-fourth.** Eleven miles of fen road and four bad miles of March road walked and measured by three men; the first break, forty yards of gone ground, put in and taken out; the second break, a quarter mile of running sand with a soft rise; the carters' man, who can put one loaded cart over and cannot put one back; Wil Tam, two shillings for a cold rivet and nothing for a tire; a levy at four times what it was, thirteen pounds four received and four pounds sixteen not received and not remitted; sixteen shillings spent and nothing moved; and a bridge with no return is a road with crates on it. **An argument. Closes with the sentence.**
3. **The sixteenth, Ch 84, the twenty-ninth.** Nine items read as nine items; the use log found to have been never a charter condition; the return main to specification withdrawn from the motion and entered as a fact of twenty-two rods of eight inch; seven items voted and four hands for and three against, and the engineer of record among the three and the reader of the book among the four; the third of the three conditions not described and not to be described before the first examination held after; the charter lapsing at midnight on a date nobody chose. **A record and not an argument, and the sentence is off it, and the reason is written underneath in the engineer's own hand so that the chain cannot be seen to have dropped one.**
4. **The seventeenth, Ch 88, the ninth.** The schedule and its declared and measured figures; twelve crates written down and six carried and six not; terms with the second clause struck and returned; two refusals entered without their terms; a sowing entered in the seventh column that goes to Cray and not to the far end; a charter lapsed on a date nobody chose and seven practices kept by a vote of four to three; a notice of four lines on the left of a door; a clerk's letter of four points in which a route over ground the district maintains is a class of use and terms are coming and a fee is not mentioned; a carter's question about a column; a Sunday reading with one person in the yard; and a woman on the low dry with a stone on a sheet of lining paper with forty-one words on it that she cannot read. **An argument. Closes with the sentence.**

**The closing sentence, *This is worth nothing against a cut*, stands on fourteen of the seventeen and is off the sixth, the eighth and the sixteenth — the same pattern as at the end of Batch 0003, the two earlier omissions being records and not arguments and both saying so underneath in the engineer's own hand, and the third being the third record this volume has produced. No later writer may add a note that carries the sentence without saying whether it is a record or an argument, and may not remove one.**

## The Fieldbook panel: one spent, in Chapter 82, and the allowance

**Spent, once, in Chapter 82, on the twenty-sixth, at about the tenth hour and forty minutes, at the north ditch of the seed house ground, the instrument laid against the lashing of the eleventh willow set — one of the sets driven on the twenty-fifth for the near end of the route — with the purpose entered in the use-log line as *the return*.** Coll Renner is the licensed man and fitted his own clip; the contact was tested with a plain wire gauge and the reading of the test written down first in his own hand; **Lissa Vale wrote the use-log line first, on the page, and read it back, and Marek read it back after her, and it went in as the twelfth line** and records the contact, the hour, the licensed man, the roll and nothing whatever about what came out. Set as a block quotation of three plain lines in the volume's house format:

> A return is a second keeper, not a second cart.
>
> The first keeper gives. The second keeper sends something back.
>
> If the second end sends nothing, the first end is a road.

**It cost what it costs.** Nine seconds he could not account for any better than the eleven at the head of the node or the four on a cut face, and he says so out loud twice and both times go in the book; the four inches on the forearm hot before it was over and staying hot; the hand from the wrist down no use from the second hour; Renner carrying the case down the seed house steps because he could not get the latch with two fingers.

**Nothing in this batch quotes the three lines in any document, on any form, in any letter, in the printing, or in anything at Wrenlow, and no character quotes them as authority.** Lissa Vale gives her rule and nothing is written. **A deliberate decision a reviewer should know about: Renner reads them twice and does NOT read them out loud, and gives the reason — he read three lines out loud in this yard in a season ago with a foreman who cannot read standing at the end of the table, and he would rather be the second, and he does not want to be a man who says a thing twice in a place where a man he employs is standing.** The Ch 73 panel is therefore not repeated as a beat; the batch pays the Batch 0003 debt differently and the instrument tells them the whole of what a return is in three lines and the fen still cannot enter them.

**The Volume 02 allowance is now zero for Batch 0004, and one panel per batch is available to Batch 0005, which is the last of the volume.**

## The use log, and where it stands

**The use log is twelve lines from Chapter 82**, and it governs the instrument and the collar. The twelve lines are the eleven of Batch 0003, unchanged, and: **12. the twenty-sixth, the tenth hour and forty minutes, the north ditch of the seed house ground, the lashing of the eleventh willow set, the instrument laid against green withy, purpose the return, licensed man present and named and the roll named, the contact tested before the contact and the reading of the test written down first in Mr Renner's own hand, and nothing whatever about what came out.** **The collar was not used in this batch and no collar contact is entered.** **There is still no use log for the fieldbook, there never has been, and a later chapter may not supply one, propose one, or thank anybody for the absence.**

## Repairs made to the drafted Volume 02 Batch 0004 on a verification pass

**Provenance.** The twelve chapters, the batch summary, the four state files and `NEXT-PROMPT.md` were all already on disk and committed when this phase was dispatched, so this phase did not write the batch and did not restart it. It read all twelve chapters in full, ran the guardrail sweeps, checked the day clock against the table above, and **found four concrete defects and repaired them in place.** **No scene was replaced, no plot moved, no character changed, and no number on a form or in a document was altered except the two dates in the district's own letter, which were wrong.** The repairs are itemised because a later writer needs to know that the class of error is still live in this range and that this batch has now been audited twice.

1. **BLOCKING — Chapter 84 contradicted its own vote.** The prose said Lissa Vale **voted against** keeping the book, in a sentence that also said *the reader of the book, against keeping the book.* That is contradicted by **the same chapter** in two other places: by the vote list, which has Lissa Vale among the **four for** (Tova Reed, Lissa Vale, Anneke Roos, Dunmar Fitch), and by the sixteenth section-nine note, which records **the reader of the book among the four.** The arithmetic also settles it — seven hands, four for, three against, and exactly four names are given for. **The state file's lock and `state/current.md` were already right and said *she voted for keeping a book and then read back the engineer's vote against it*, so the chapter and the chapter summary were the two things out.** The sentence now reads: *Lissa Vale read it back without softening a word, and then voted to keep it, and there it was: the reader of the book, reading a man's vote against it aloud in a yard and putting her own hand up.* **The beat is stronger for the fix, not weaker, and the count of the batch's section-nine notes is unaffected.** Ch 84: 3,108 → **3,116** words.
2. **Ch 87's distance back to the vote was short by four days.** The man of about fifty reads the closing entry on the **seventh of next month (day 127)** and the text called his vote against the book **four days ago**; the vote was on **the twenty-ninth of last month (day 119)**, which is **eight**. Corrected to **eight days ago.** The chapter summary's version of the same fact said *five days ago* and is now **eight**.
3. **Ch 88's own letter cited a document that the batch had placed on a different day.** The four points opened by answering *a return of the fourteenth of a month two months back, to a form of the nineteenth of that month, and to a schedule received on the twenty-fifth of that month.* **Chapter 79 puts the counter-mark and the holder's return on the fourteenth of that month, which from day 129 is *last month*, and the fen's form S/10 went back on the twenty-first, not the nineteenth.** The citation is now **a return of the fourteenth of last month, to a form of the twenty-first of last month, and to a schedule received on the twenty-fifth of last month**, which is days 104, 111 and 115, and every one of them is in the batch. **A district office citing its own file is now citing the batch's own paper, which is what makes point two — the entry of this holding as a local holder — the answer to a thing the district already had in front of it.**
4. **Two intervals in Ch 88 that did not reconcile with any date, and one date that did not reconcile with Ch 79.** *It took four days over four months* described a file whose first document is twenty-five days old; it is now **four days over four weeks** (days 104, 109, 115, 129), which is true. Tova Reed's *a question I asked on the sixth of last month* is now **the fourteenth of last month**, which is the day Ch 79 says she asked it. And Lissa Vale's reason for reading the schedule out on the Sunday said the chalk carter had stood at the gate **four days ago**; he stood there on **the second (day 122)** and the Sunday is the seventh (day 127), which is **five**. Ch 88: 2,601 → **2,598** words.

**The day clock, as re-verified after the repairs.** Nothing in the repairs touched a day number, a weekday, the month boundary or the tin. **The batch is still days 105–129, the fifteenth of one month through the ninth of the next, crossing one month boundary after Chapter 84, with the charter lapsing at midnight on day 119, a Saturday, and the first of next month a Monday on day 121, and the convoy moving on a Saturday on day 126.** **The March's tin is still *day minus nineteen* and still stands at 110 days on the ninth.** **No month is named anywhere in Chapters 77–88 and no document in the batch carries one**, and the four calendar month names that were in the first draft remain cut. **The count of the readings of the seventh column still reconciles exactly: ten on the twenty-sixth, sixteen on the second, seventeen on the third when it was filled.** The three-week gap between the second and the third of next month in the return column's own count is the number of days in the interval and it is right.

**Word count after the repairs: 33,104 words by `wc -w` over the twelve chapter files including their chapter headings.** Chapter range **2,598–3,116**, every chapter inside the 2,550–3,120 band, nothing under the floor and nothing over the ceiling. **Chapter 84 at 3,116 is four words under a ceiling and a later writer must not lengthen it.** Chapters 86 (2,915) and 77 (3,009) are the two nearest a ceiling and should be trimmed rather than padded.

## Stage and contact accounting after Volume 02 Batch 0004

- **Marek is Stage 2, Grafthand, and this batch does not move him.** The stage is one small graft old. He can make a small reliable graft between living tissue and a limited local branch and he can keep two or three active links; a fourth fails. **He still cannot draw, has no bead, no anchor work, no bridge above a local conduit, no seedheart, no caretaker link and no reading of the deep archive, and the touchroot sense is unchanged and still unreliable.** **He laid a collar on a wetted lot not at all in this batch, and a crew building a bridge is not a stage, and no number in this batch may be used to move him.**
- **The rootmark is present and unchanged: four inches along the inside of the left forearm, branching twice, permanent, recorded in this holding's book by Coll Renner under his own hand in Chapter 57.** In this batch it was hot before the Ch 82 contact was over and stayed hot, and the hand from the wrist down was no use from the second hour. **Nobody in this yard wrote the word *became* and nobody may.**
- **Contacts behind him: the four of Volume 01, unchanged in kind and cost; the one at the head of the node in Ch 63 entered as a contact and not a finding; the one in Ch 73 against the wood of a collar bead; and the one in Ch 82, the instrument against the withy of the eleventh willow set, nine seconds, at a licensed man's hands with a licensed man's clip, the purpose entered as the return, costing the mark and a day of the hand, and producing three lines that cannot be checked and cannot be entered.**
- **No draw and no return by rootlight in this batch, and none anywhere in Volume 02.** The panel's second line is the reason the volume does not need one.

## New locks from the drafted Volume 02 Batch 0004

- **A seed-route bridge is a route with a return, and the return is the compost line, and the compost line is empty.** That is the volume's whole Movement 4 and it is now a fact on a door with a measured figure in it. **The batch's sentence, and it is on a door: six crates out in a month and none back, and six is a fifth of thirty.** A later chapter may not quietly raise the measured figure and may not fill the seventh column with anything that is not a thing coming back down the path.
- **A return does not have to be compost.** What comes back down a path is whatever goes along it and comes back: not a kindness, not a gift, not a mood, **a weight, at two ends, on a day, with a name against it.** The fen's return is a sowing and it goes to Cray. **No chapter may call it a solution to the three hundred and ninety-nine hundredweight and Ch 86 says so on the page and puts it in capitals.**
- **A schedule with a blank in it is a schedule anybody may copy including the blank, and that is the point of it.** A column that has never been filled is not a broken promise; it is a line anybody may write on. **The form has no number on it on purpose, and a later chapter may not put one on it.**
- **A body with no charter can be a party to nothing, and is therefore the only kind of body a settlement with no charter can be party to.** This is the whole of the road between two places and it is not a legal foundation; **it is the absence of a floor, and it is the only reason the arrangement cannot be enforced, and that is the only protection the far end has.**
- **Making a body with no charter a party to a document is the only way of making it liable to one, and the only protection it has is that it can refuse and cannot be examined.** Clause two is gone for that reason and clause four is the price of the document and stays. **A later chapter may not make a settlement with no charter sign anything, hold a copy, answer on a record, or be examinable about one.**
- **The far end holds no copy of the terms, has signed nothing, has been asked to sign nothing, is on the schedule, and has had its name kept off it in advance.** The schedule's second end is *eleven households, keeper not named*, and it was written before anybody had been there, **which is the only honest way a place can be written about in advance, and it is the fen's whole substitute for a survey.**
- **A printed record is worth nothing to a person who has never been handed a form, and the only thing that works is a person standing in front of her saying it.** That is what Marek goes to the far end for, and Ch 62's rule does not reach it. **A later chapter may not have anybody at the far end read, sign, quote or refer to a document the fen has printed, and the certificate of forty-one words on lining paper with no printed head is now a document class.**
- **Being readable is a position and not a virtue, and the holding is not thanked for it and cannot be.** The man of about fifty's closing sentence of the batch is the standing position: **a stone on a piece of paper she cannot read is worth more than the six crates, and the fen is to be careful about how pleased it is.**
- **Every Crown act in this batch is correct, timely and structural, and no office has lied, obstructed or acted corruptly, and no villain may be inserted to fix any of it.** Four acts: the four-line enquiry that discovered a settlement by asking a question about it, because a letter is a record; form S/10 with a six-digit box that cannot be filled in, because ground the district carries in schedule is ground with no holding number; the four-line notice that the node is not shown to be maintained; and the four points of the ninth, **the third of which says that a route over ground the district maintains is a class of use and that terms will be sent in the ordinary course and will name a rate and a class of use.** **That is Movement 5's door and it is not a tax, and a later chapter may not have arrived at it any other way.**
- **The charter is gone and is not renewed, not varied, not referred, and no officer obstructed it, and seven practices are kept by choice.** **The open book, the monthly examination, the monthly report, the standing pressure log, the use log, the seed annex and the copy to Cray are the seven.** **The return main to specification was not kept by choice, because this body cannot promise a Crown specification, and is entered as a fact of twenty-two rods of eight inch.** **The use log was never a condition of anything, and the fen kept it for a season before anybody found out, and the vote is the paperwork catching up with an instinct.** A later chapter may not treat the expiry as a defeat and may not treat it as a release.
- **The third of the three conditions in clause three is now untested, unowned, unnamed, and its test date has been and gone.** It will be described at an examination, on a form, to whoever holds the node on that day. **No chapter may name it, soften it, or have anybody in Tarrow Fen guess at it, and no chapter may have the fen write and ask for it.**
- **Lissa Vale is not forgiven and is not thanked.** She made a second refusal to keep a document, she was right about it, she read the schedule out on a Sunday that was not one of the days she had set, she entered the return column and added nothing to it, and **she voted for keeping a book she has kept for thirty-one years and then read back the vote of the engineer of record against it.** Her refusal of the fourth of a month of Batch 0002 is hers, is not Sera Quill's, is not her own fourth, and is not numbered.
- **Nia is not in this batch.** Not in a room, not in a letter, not by report. **The letter is in the open drawer of the seed-house table, sealed, and it has now been unopened and undiscussed across five batches and about eighty days of season, and no chapter in this batch wrote down why and no chapter had anybody ask.** One reference to her is to her lashing, and it is a way of doing a thing, and it is entered as a cost of a hundred and eight pounds a year, and nobody in the yard says anything about it.
- **Sera Quill is not in this batch, is not asked about Brinewake, and nobody has asked about her fourth refusal.** It is unnumbered and unasked and may not be numbered. **The volume's first licensed witness is still Coll Renner and it is still not Sera Quill, and no chapter may have her simply arrive.**
- **The Thornwild settlement is not named, the long line is not used, quoted or entered, and the word *seat* does not appear anywhere in Chapters 77–88.** Ivo Tarrant's four men do not come back, are not a rescue, and are not brought back to dig anything; **his name and the offer are named in a yard at the second break in Ch 81 and the ditch is still not dug and forty-one houses are still on a bed this holding killed.** Marda Vole does not appear. Dunmar's *none known* is spent. The sealed envelope is never opened. Osvalde Bream does not come back.
- **The collie is not interpreted and may not be used as a symbol, the weir animal does not return, and Marek has still not walked the low quarter's own map and the copy of it is still on this holding's table unopened and entered as not of this holding.**
- **Kellan Rusk is not in this batch and is not named in it.** **A charter broker names three candidates in a district for the post of somebody a district can serve a form on, and one of the three is described and not named: a private charterholder at a pump house with a gauge below the split.** That is the whole of his presence and the words *became* and *redeemed* are both prohibited for him in this volume.
- **No new named member of the cooperative.** The man of about fifty and the man of about seventy are held by function and may not be given names without recording it. **Wenna Roke and Wil Tam are the batch's two new named people, and neither is a member of anything, and the crew is unchanged: Ilsa Kern, Jonas Byre, Anneke Roos, Gael Senn, Lissa Vale, Dunmar Fitch and Marek Vale, plus Tova Reed as the trust's steward and the two men held by function.** **Seven hands voted on the twenty-ninth and the man of about seventy was not in the yard, and the prose does not state the body's headcount and this file does not supply one, because Ch 74 of Batch 0003 says *the body is six of us* and then names two more, and that is Ch 74's business and a reviewer of Ch 74's, not this batch's.** A later writer may not have the count of the body rise or fall as a convenience.
- **The leak is still a leak at five-eighths of an inch, twice measured, two rules, at the seventh hour, pump on, into the housing and not into the pit, unmoved under a full draw, and it was measured on the first of next month by a woman who has no appointment to measure it, and the fen did not use a charter's expiry to stop measuring it.** The beat is logged **twelve** times, on the first of next month, cause not established, licensed person present none, and nothing was written underneath it. **The 399 hundredweight is carried forward and not discharged, the compost line has read nothing put in, none available, for three months running, the bed is at three and a half degrees, the tin is unmoved at 110 days on the ninth, and the graft is fed about a twentieth.**

## Proposed canon changes recorded, not silently applied — Volume 02 Batch 0004

Batch 0004 added eleven things by drafting. All are local detail, mechanisms, forms, prices, places, people or records, and none changes a world rule, a power rule, a relationship lock, or the ending:

1. **A seed-route bridge as a public schedule with a return column.** The bridge is built of fiber beds, ditch work, willow sets, a compost bay, carts, ruts and crews, and it exists as a piece of paper with seven columns and no number on it, nailed on a well house door, with a measured capacity of six crates a month under a declared capacity of thirty. **This is a new document class and the volume's fourth, after the open book, the certificate of origin and the cutting sheet.**
2. **The terms of a route, six clauses, with the second struck out and the fourth kept.** A new instrument class, and the standing of a body with no charter inside a document, is now settled in prose: **it is examinable and it can refuse, and the protection is that it can refuse and the exposure is that it is examinable, and the fen chose the exposure deliberately.**
3. **A route carrying seed over ground the district carries in schedule is a class of use, and classes of use carry terms, and terms will name a rate.** **This is a new Crown category and it is Movement 5's mechanism, and it is a category and not a villain and not a rate yet, and the rate does not exist in this batch.**
4. **Wrenlow** — eleven plots, eleven households, no gate, a well in, and a keeper of thirty-nine with a list on lining paper under a stone. **The far end of the route is now a place with a name, a count and a person, and it is not the Thornwild settlement and no chapter may confuse the two.**
5. **Wenna Roke** — thirty-nine, keeps seed for eleven households, cannot read a form and says so, keeps a short line alive in eleven plots, and has no column for where anything came from. **She is not a member of anything, she is not rescued, she is not redeemed, and her two refusals are entered without their terms.**
6. **The compost bay and the two boards and the number of days.** A physical object that is the batch's standing image, and the number cut into the wood is the only figure in the volume that goes up.
7. **The March's dry line is in the ground on this fen, four inches high, in six trays, in a bed at three and a half degrees, and the sowing closed on the twenty-ninth of last month.** The eleventh of the eleven cuttings is not a cutting in a tin any more; **it is a sowing, and what comes off it is a return, and that is the first time in this volume that a return has existed that is not a hundredweight of compost.** The line itself is not recovered and is not paid for and is in no book.
8. **A charter that has lapsed is a body with no charter, and the six things it was doing are now six choices.** The canonical status of every charter condition in the volume changes on day 119, and none of them stops being true. **A later chapter may not have the district reinstate, waive or refer any of them, and may not have anybody in the fen stop one of them because the charter is dead.**
9. **The return main to specification is now nobody's business and is a fact of twenty-two rods of eight inch.** A Crown specification that will never be enforced against anybody, entered as a fact rather than a motion because a body with no charter cannot promise a bore. **This is the volume's standing example of a condition that cannot be complied with by anybody and is not a defeat, and it points directly at Movement 5's rate.**
10. **A cutting sheet.** A trade form that does not exist, written by a buyer who has been told there is no column for it and has put a third column on it anyway. **It is the same class of defect as the trust's missing seventh column and it is now in the hands of both ends of the trade, and it has not been answered.**
11. **Two new people in the seed house's own orbit who are not members: Wenna Roke and Wil Tam.** Recorded here and not in the crew list, and the crew is unchanged at eight names of which two are held by function.

**None of the eleven changes a world rule, a power rule, a relationship lock, or the ending.** The ending is untouched: Chancellor Iona Vey remains the final antagonist, the Rootway is not a mind, the rootmark is one and permanent and no second appears, and the volume's question — **who altered the maps and the seed records enough to make a bridge between this fen and the dry Salt March legal, and who was paid for the alteration** — is still unanswered. **What this batch added to the shape and not to the content is a name, a count of eleven, a keeper who cannot read a form, a form with a six-digit box that cannot be filled in, a class of use, a cutting sheet, and a seed-route bridge that is a fifth of what it announced. A name and a class of use are not a map, a printer and a reason.**
